## Chapter 116
### Concerning why if a [Nail] is cut the Person shall not be sick; but if a Limb is cut he shall immediately be pained.

*Heavily lacunose chapter preserving a body-cosmos correspondence teaching: body↔earth, limbs↔trees, varieties of trees↔varieties of body parts (some regrow when cut, others do not). This is systematic body-cosmos mapping consistent with the substrate pattern identified in Ch.70. The teaching purity score of 0.0 is misleading — the substrate vocabulary here uses common terms (body, earth, trees) rather than the marked zoomorphic/metal terms the pipeline detects. The STRUCTURE is pure correspondential mapping: both sides stay within the natural/cosmic system. Dialogue frame is dominant by paragraph count (5 of 7) but the two teaching paragraphs contain the bulk of the word count. No editorial bridge, institutional, or exhortation layers detected — this chapter appears to be substrate teaching wrapped in dialogue frame with no later editorial extension.*

why when limbs are cut from the body of the person he shall be pained? While if the hair on the head of the person's body should be shaved, and [the nai]ls o]f his hands and feet be cut, he does not notice it [...] Some of these even now shall grow an[d ...] again. Just as the na[ils ...] so the hair shall grow and [...] cut a finger, he in [...] on the limbs of the [bod]y that you [.. ⟨p.281⟩ ...] him [...] since this time to the [... ...] the body ag[a]in.

th[at the] body is like the earth, wh[ile these] limbs als[o ...] of it are like the trees. There are varieties [...] of tree you shall find growing on the earth. They ar[is]e and grow again and stand firm. They have not [... ...] Again, there are others that if they are cut shall not [grow ...] and they grow, as they are li[ke ...] which shall be cut and grow [...] and [...] they too that occur [...] they shall be cut and not grow, resembling [... neither] grow nor shall they [... ...] it [sh]all please him on [...] and grows [...] in impurity [... ...] from him. This flesh that is corrupt [... ⟨p.282⟩ ...] in the liv[in]g flesh and they [... ...] are somewhat sick.

**Temporal observations:**
- ¶1: Standard dialogue frame formula: 'Once again one of the disciples questioned the apostle. He says to him:' — purely formulaic with no cosmological content.
- ¶2: The question defines the teaching structure: it identifies the phenomenon (differential pain and regrowth in body parts) that the body-cosmos correspondence will explain. The observation itself — that nails/hair regrow when cut but fingers/limbs do not — is the empirical basis for the tree-variety mapping in ¶5. This is a substantive cosmological question in the Persian pedagogical tradition. Very fragmentary in the second half.
- ¶3: Pure plea formula: 'I beseech you, my master, that you might instruct me' — no cosmological content, only honorific address requesting instruction.
- ¶4: Pure dialogue attribution: 'Then the a[postle] speaks to him:' — standard response-introduction formula.
- ¶5: Core body-cosmos correspondence: body↔earth, limbs↔trees. The teaching then classifies tree varieties by regrowth behavior — some grow back when cut, others do not — and maps these back to body parts. Both sides of the mapping stay within the natural/cosmic system. This is the same body-cosmos correspondence tradition attested in Ch.70. Despite heavy lacunae, the structure is clear: systematic classification of body-part types through tree-variety analogy. The final lines about 'impurity' and 'flesh that is corrupt' / 'living flesh' suggest the teaching also addressed the mixture of living and dead substance in the body — a substrate concern.
- ¶6: Formulaic closure: 'Behold, I have taught you about this lesson for which you have asked me' — standard dialogue-frame conclusion wrapping the teaching unit.
- ¶7: Narrative closure: 'the disciple heard this homily... he sat down' — standard session-ending formula. Very fragmentary.

Gardner notes the chapter likely continued onto a now-destroyed leaf. about these people who shall [ ... ... ] and their soul is released from [their body ... ... ] if he shall fall upon [ ... ]

The [sec]ond [ ... ] if he shall fall [ ... ... ] one [ ... ... ] one hour [ ... ... ] others who shall [ ... ... ] and they remain. They shall delay until they [ ... ... ] he is freed, and they delay to come toward [ ... ... ]

which names shall be freed, as they [ ... ... ] release them.

- ¶2: Enumeration marker '[sec]ond' indicates this is part of a numbered teaching sequence about categories of soul release. Zero substrate vocabulary score reflects diagnostic term loss to lacunae, not absence of substrate character.
- ¶3: Follow-up question wrapped in dialogue frame. 'Which names shall be freed' is a substantive cosmological question about categories of beings subject to liberation — 'names' likely refers to classes of beings in the cosmic system. Very fragmentary.
- ¶4: Pure dialogue attribution formula: 'Then he says to the [...]'. Too fragmentary for any teaching content to be discernible. The transition formula marks the beginning of the teacher's response, but the response itself is lost to lacunae.

## Chapter 119
### [ ... ]

*The chapter preserves a three-name cosmological teaching about the First Man ('approved', 'righteous', 'only begotten') in ¶3–5, which is genuine substrate — systematic, impersonal explanation of cosmic epithets with both mapping sides staying within the cosmic system (aeons of light, the enemy, the Father, five sons). Paragraph 8 contains a substrate description of the First Man's cosmic function (standing firm in Darkness, purifying, freeing). The editorial layer then applies these cosmic attributes to the community (¶6, ¶9–10), with ¶4 showing institutional vocabulary ('catechumen') inserted into substrate teaching. Heavy lacunae throughout limit confidence. Gardner's synopsis confirms the Christological/exhortative editorial purpose. Moderate editorial fatigue with exhortation surging in the second half.*

⟨p.285⟩[ ... ] and the [righte]o[usness ... ] is more than [the ... ] the voice through them.

This one, whom they called "approved" [ ... ... ] they approved him in the midst of the ae[ons of] light. These numerous multitudes set him a[part], and he came forth against the enemy. They gave this name [ ... ... ] this matter [ ... ... ] who are numerous, that are in the aeons of light and who [are without] measure or number.

This one, [whom they called] "righteous", because he is the first righteous one who had no sin amongst all of the aeons that belong to the light and the thought [ ... ] they knew him, so that [ ... becau]se he is the first [wh]o obe[y]ed his Father, who had sent him with five sons [ ... ] perdition.

This one also [whom they called] "only begotten", because he was established [ ... ] pour [ ... ] enemy.

[the o]nly begot-ten also from gold and silver and all the [ ... ] of the universe.

⟨p.286⟩ [The F]irst Man, himself stood firm in the [midst] of all the worlds of Darkness. He did not love [the] worlds of Darkness, nor did he lust for them! Rather, h[is] thought and love for the household of his people is in h[i]m. Anything else that the enemy [ ... ] with his violent de[e]ds. Behold, (the First Man) toils and struggles; so that (the enemy) would be separat[e from] them and be cast out from them. And he purifies them and ma[ke]s them f[r]ee, and makes them as they were from the beginning.

**Temporal observations:**
- ¶1: Extremely fragmentary. Mentions 'righteousness' which connects to the chapter's three-name teaching about the First Man. No editorial markers present. Tentatively substrate but classification is very uncertain due to lacunae.
- ¶2: 'The apostle speaks to them about these four...' is formulaic dialogue-frame attribution. The reference to 'these four' might gesture at substantive content but is too fragmentary to extract. 'Because they had recounted to him from [the hour] and time' is narrative framing.
- ¶3: First element of a three-name cosmological teaching (approved, righteous, only begotten). Both sides of the mapping stay within the cosmic system: the aeons of light approve the First Man and set him apart against the enemy. Systematic, impersonal, expository. Classic substrate structure.
- ¶4: Second element of the three-name teaching. The substrate describes the First Man as 'righteous' — without sin among the aeons of light, obeying his Father, sent with five sons (the five elements). The institutional vocabulary 'catechumen' (flagged at 4.92) is a clear editorial gloss: the editor maps the cosmic obedience of the First Man onto the institutional role of catechumen. 'Five sons' is core Manichaean cosmological nomenclature for the First Man's emanations.
- ¶5: Third element of the three-name teaching. Very fragmentary but the naming formula ('This one also [whom they called]') and the cosmological content (established against the enemy) match ¶3 and ¶4 exactly. Both sides stay within the cosmic system.
- ¶6: Clear pivot to application: 'So now, I tell you, my brothers and my limbs' — classic exhortation formula. The three cosmic names (approved, righteous, only begotten) are now applied to the community: 'after the likeness of the First Man.' One side of the mapping leaves the cosmic system and reaches into the editor's community. Contains institutional vocabulary 'catechumen' (×2). Exhortation score 5.71 confirms. This is where cosmos→cosmos mapping becomes cosmos→community application.
- ¶7: Very fragmentary. The gold and silver reference is a potential metal-body correspondence (substrate marker: metal mapped to cosmic being). 'Only begotten from gold and silver and all the [...] of the universe' appears to describe the cosmic constitution of the First Man or light beings. However, this is wrapped in applicative framing ('your race,' 'you,' 'according to flesh'). Low confidence in extraction due to heavy lacunae.
- ¶8: Strong substrate content: the First Man standing firm in the worlds of Darkness, not loving them, his thought directed to his people, toiling against the enemy, purifying and freeing, restoring to the original state. Both sides of every mapping stay within the cosmic system — this describes WHAT the First Man IS and DOES. The 'your father' address and 'Just as' comparative structure are editorial framing that prepare for the application in ¶9. Capitalized 'Darkness' as it names the cosmic realm/entity.
- ¶9: 'You too, so long as you stand firm' — direct application of the First Man's cosmic steadfastness to the community. 'After the likeness of your living father' explicitly marks this as imitation-teaching. The cosmic mapping now reaches outside the system to address the audience's behavior in 'this body.'
- ¶10: Continues the applicative voice. 'May they discover the remembrance of the household of his people' — the cosmic concept ('household of his people' from ¶8) is now being applied devotionally. 'Night and day' is temporal rather than cosmological. Fragmentary but consistent with the exhortative closure pattern.
- ¶11: Formulaic closing: disciples hear, rejoice, give glory, praise, make obeisance. Editorial seam correctly flagged — bridge connective with institutional vocabulary ('teacher,' 'disciples'). Classic dialogue-frame closure that wraps the teaching session. The structure flag 'formulaic closing' at the chapter level confirms this.

## Chapter 120
### Concerning the Two Essences

*This chapter is overwhelmingly polemical, not expository. The title names a substrate topic (the two essences = primordial duality) but the chapter uses that topic as a weapon against monotheism rather than systematically mapping it. Mani is preaching/arguing, not teaching cosmological correspondences. The only substrate fragment is the brief naming of the two essences and their paired opposites in the opening paragraph. The remainder is direct argumentative address ('you'), rhetorical catalogues of evil, citations of Jesus's tree parable as proof-text, and eschatological warnings. Teaching purity 0.000 confirms this. No systematic mapping (face→realm, metal→body, taste→world) appears anywhere. The five trees are mentioned in ¶6 but as citation within polemic, not as expounded teaching. Heavy lacunae throughout make the chapter additionally fragmentary.*

the two essences that are present at the beginning [ ... ] the light and the darkness, that which is good and that which is evi[], life and] death.

**Temporal observations:**
- ¶1: The naming of the two primordial essences with three paired opposites (light/darkness, good/evil, life/death) is a cosmological-correspondential statement — it maps the fundamental duality through parallel registers. This is the chapter's only cosmos→cosmos mapping. The rest of the chapter uses this claim polemically.
- ¶2: Direct address to 'you, however, the creatures' — argumentative rhetoric against monotheists. The cosmological claim (one vs. two essences) is being APPLIED to a contemporary theological debate, not being systematically taught. One side of the mapping reaches into the audience's religious position. Heavy lacunae obscure some content.
- ¶3: Rhetorical catalogue of evils directed at audience ('So, tell me...'). The list includes contemporary practices: 'worshipping of idols,' 'Mammon,' 'fornication in which people revel.' These are not systematic cosmic-domain mappings but a polemical inventory reaching into the contemporary moral world. The 'evil spirits that are like the night' approaches cosmic language but is embedded in the rhetorical frame. The question 'who cast them in the heart of people' is argumentative, not expository.
- ¶4: Continues the polemical argument via reductio ad absurdum: if evils came from God, why don't you do them? Direct 'you' address throughout. 'Two woes' and 'you forgive their sins' — argumentative rhetoric mapping cosmic claim→contemporary religious practice. No cosmological mapping present.
- ¶5: Continues argument. References 'his beloved son' and 'all the apostles' — institutional/apostolic authority claims. 'The bitter trees that give not fruit' and 'the hard earth' are citations of the tree parable (Gospel proof-text), not systematic cosmological teaching. Gardner confirms this is 'the favourite proof-text.' The vehicle is citation ('saying that'), not exposition. Heavy lacunae obscure the five-fold structure that Gardner notes.
- ¶6: 'Once again he says' — citation formula. The 'five trees' reference is tantalizing (five-fold cosmological teaching exists as substrate elsewhere), but here it functions as proof-text within polemic, not as systematic teaching being expounded. 'His beloved son and his holy spirit and the entire kingdom' — theological/institutional language. The paragraph argues that attributing evil to God is blasphemous, using the tree teaching as evidence.
- ¶7: Eschatological warning/threat directed at audience: 'You will be condemned.' References 'his beloved son and his holy spirit at the last day, at his advent' — classic devotional-eschatological closure language. Direct address, future judgment, institutional theological vocabulary.
- ¶8: Final eschatological warning: 'You shall come from your body and see these things that I have recounted to you.' 'The Judge of truth, the one who shall not favour anyone' — devotional closure formula. First person ('I have recounted to you') anchors to the editorial Mani-as-speaker frame. Closes the polemical chapter with a judgment scene.

## Chapter 121
### Concerning the Sect of the Basket

*Chapter has a clear two-part structure: (1) paragraphs 1-4 are a narrative encounter with a presbyter from a non-Manichaean sect, entirely institutional/editorial/exhortatory material including polemic and a beatitude identification of 'basket' with 'holy church'; (2) paragraphs 5-6 contain heavily fragmentary but recognizable cosmological teaching — cosmic architecture (pillars, disk, sea), cosmic beings (Mind, Youth, Porter, five sons of the Man, Adamant of Light), and cosmic enumerations (thirty-six, twenty-two, twelve, seven, four). The substrate is NOT dominant — it survives only in the latter, very damaged portion. Teaching purity is low (0.254). The editorial fatigue pattern is inverted here — substrate increases in the second half (+5.39), suggesting the cosmological teaching was appended or preserved at the end while the first half was fully replaced by narrative polemic. Gardner's uncertainty about the chapter's coherence is justified — the connection between the sect discussion and the cosmological catalogue is unclear.*

The [...] and the great [...] and the great Mind that is [...] this Youth that is with the [...] thirty-six angels [...] twenty-two angels [...] but [...] of the twenty-two [...] ⟨p.290⟩ the seven pillars that stan[d ...] the sea that surrounds the universe [...] four angels that are distributed to the [...] universe, supporting the mightiness [...] below, which is the earth of all the heavens [...] these other five gods, the sons of the Man, they that [...] the universe is stood firm [...] the summons and the obedience [...] the splendour of the [...] the Adamant of Light [...] they are become enemies [...] they guide it.

The third also, [...] below, there are twelve powers standing firm there. However, the number of the twelve images [...] the three great pillars of mightiness that [...] pillars supporting the disk [...] the other three [...] they are the garments that the Living Spirit [...] he put them at that time [...] that threw them, together with [...] which is displayed therein [...] this [...] perfect before [...] great Porter [...] in his power [...] these are the three great [...] against [...] are in the midst of people [...] knows [...] entrusts his soul to them [...].

**Temporal observations:**
- ¶1: Pure narrative frame formula: 'Once again, on one of the occasions, as our enlightener is sitting' followed by narrative setting in Babylon. High dialogue_frame (15.52) and institutional (17.24) scores confirm. 'A presbyter belonging to the [sect] of the basket' and 'worshipper of idols' are narrative identifications, not cosmological teaching.
- ¶2: Heavily lacunose discussion of the sect's name and practices — fruit hanging from trees, grapes, vine, apples, basket. All register scores at zero. This describes a specific sect's identity and practices (possibly fruitarian, per Gardner). No cosmological mapping; the fruit/tree language here is about literal agricultural practice, not cosmic correspondence.
- ¶3: Opens with dialogue frame attribution ('The apostle speaks to the presbyter of the sect of the basket:'). The content is a rhetorical argument using simile (universe 'like a tree', person 'like fruit') addressed to 'you' (the presbyter). This is polemic directed at a specific sect — one side of the mapping reaches into the presbyter's contemporary situation ('how then do you call yourself the son of the basket'). Not systematic cosmological mapping but rhetorical use of analogy for institutional polemic. The concept of being 'entangled in the universe' is cosmological vocabulary repurposed for argument.
- ¶4: Beatitude formula ('B[less]ed [...] the children of the basket') is the structural marker of the exhortation layer. Wraps strongly institutional content: alms-giving, renunciation of possessions ('from the houses, from the gold and silver'), and the climactic institutional identification 'which is the holy church!' Functions as devotional closure to the polemic section (¶2-3). 'He raised his hand to his disciples' is a narrative gesture transitioning from addressing the presbyter to addressing the community.
- ¶5: Dense cosmological catalogue despite heavy lacunae. Identifiable substrate entities: the great Mind (Nous), the Youth (Third Messenger figure), thirty-six and twenty-two angels (cosmic enumerations), seven pillars (cosmic architecture), the sea surrounding the universe (cosmic geography), four supporting angels, the earth of all the heavens, five gods/sons of the Man (five sons of Primal Man), the summons and the obedience (the Call and Answer pair), the Adamant of Light. Substrate score 10.83 confirms. 'Holy church' disrupts an otherwise cosmic list — classic editorial seam inserting institutional identity into substrate enumeration.
- ¶6: Continues the cosmic architecture catalogue. 'The third also' is an enumeration marker continuing from ¶5's teaching sequence. Substrate entities: twelve powers, twelve images (cosmic correspondences), three great pillars of mightiness (cosmic architecture), pillars supporting the disk (the cosmic disk/earth-plate), the Living Spirit (Mihryazd — major cosmic emanation), the great Porter (Atlas-figure supporting the worlds). Low substrate score (2.02) likely reflects vocabulary pipeline limitations with heavily lacunose text. The ending 'are in the midst of people [...] knows [...] entrusts his soul to them' is ambiguous — could be editorial bridge reaching into the human world, but too fragmentary to determine. Preserved with uncertainty noted.

## Chapter 122
### Concerning the 'Assent' and the 'Amen'

*This chapter is predominantly institutional/editorial material explaining the cosmic significance of Manichaean liturgical practices (assent and amen). The substrate core is limited to ¶2-4: a compact teaching that maps assent→summons and amen→obedience (answer), then describes how these cosmic entities functioned as portals for the First Man's redemption and as purifiers of the living soul. From ¶5 onward, the teaching applies these cosmic archetypes to congregational worship practice — describing liturgical sealing, the formation of ascending images from collective sound, healing power, and overcoming temptation. All of this later material has one side of its mapping reaching into institutional practice ('the congregation', 'great crowds of people', 'the elect', 'their alms'). The text is heavily damaged throughout with extensive lacunae, especially ¶4 and ¶10-16. Substrate vocabulary is entirely concentrated in the first half (8.71→0.00), confirming the core teaching ends early. Gardner notes this chapter was completed from Funk's working text beyond the published edition.*

What is the 'assent' and the 'amen'?

The assent and the amen have come about from these great ones of the powers. They were given the name as the assent belongs to the summons and the amen belongs to the answer.

Just as the summons and the obedience are the ones that opened the portals of mercy and entreaty for the First Man, so they also became portals for the Mother of Life and the Father of the living. The First Man sought and found his redeemer. And also the Mother of Life and the Father of the living sought [...] this runner, who came forth from them, who is the First Man; but the summons and the obedience became the portals for them.

they have become portals for the aeons of greatness towards the First Man, and the soul that was conjoined with death. They became helpers [...] of the First Man during his ascent. They gave him [...] the Father of Life and the Mother of Life [...]. They are purifiers of the living soul, being helpers and bestowers of remembrance for it, be it either in [...] in the tree or in the creation of flesh [...]

**Temporal observations:**
- ¶1: The question 'What is the assent and the amen?' is substantive — it asks about the cosmic NATURE of these entities, and the answer (¶2-3) maps them to cosmic archetypes. The surrounding material about blessing and prayer is institutional context. Institutional score 4.55 confirms liturgical vocabulary.
- ¶2: This is the key correspondential mapping: assent→summons, amen→answer (obedience). Both sides stay within the cosmic system — liturgical terms mapped to cosmic entities. This establishes the archetype that the rest of the chapter will apply.
- ¶3: Pure substrate. Highest substrate score in the chapter (26.51). Describes cosmic process: summons/obedience as portals enabling the First Man's redemption. All entities (First Man, Mother of Life, Father of the living) and functions (portals, seeking, finding, redemption) are within the cosmic system. 'Runner' (the First Man) is a cosmological designation. No audience address, no institutional reference.
- ¶4: Mixed paragraph with clear substrate in the first portion (substrate 19.83, institutional 10.34). The cosmic teaching — portals for the aeons, helpers during the First Man's ascent, purifiers of the living soul — continues ¶3's cosmological narrative. 'The soul that was conjoined with death' is substrate soul-body binding language. The transition to 'the holy church' is the editorial seam where the cosmic archetype is applied to the institutional present.
- ¶5: Liturgical practice instruction: describes what happens when one blesses and responds with assent/amen ('shall seal the blesser'). One side of the mapping reaches into communal worship. Institutional score 4.44. Note the page marker ⟨p.292⟩ appears here but the chapter opened at ⟨p.291⟩ and ¶4 was ⟨p.293⟩ — possible folio displacement in the manuscript.
- ¶6: Highest institutional score in the chapter (18.18). Describes congregational liturgical practice: 'when the congregation will beseech an entreaty with a question, and they all answer and say verily and amen.' One side of every mapping reaches into communal worship ('the congregation', 'they all answer'). Clearly institutional.
- ¶7: Pedagogical formula 'Happen you know that' (appears also in ¶9) introduces teaching about assent/amen as 'a great person.' While the personification has cosmological resonance ('new birth daily, very good and honoured in the presence of the living ones above'), the entire passage is framed as audience address ('all of you', 'you rejoice', 'came to you profitably') explaining what the community's liturgical practice produces. The cosmic effect is triggered by institutional practice. Dialogue_frame score 6.67 reflects the pedagogical address.
- ¶8: Describes how collective liturgical sound forms a beautiful image that ascends to 'the land of tranquillity and peace.' The cosmic process (sound → collection → sculpting → image → ascent) is genuinely interesting cosmologically, and 'land of tranquillity and peace' is cosmic geography. However, the process is explicitly triggered by communal practice ('great crowds of people utter the assent and the amen') and concludes with 'their joy and their alms' — institutional vocabulary. One side reaches into congregational practice. Institutional score 3.09.
- ¶9: Opens third section per Gardner synopsis: assent/amen as 'a great power' for healing. 'Happen you know that' pedagogical formula (cf. ¶7). Describes praying over the sick — clearly institutional/pastoral application of cosmic power to community practice. Heavily damaged with many lacunae.
- ¶10: Very fragmentary (17 words). Continues healing theme: 'a great help comes to him from the assent and the amen.' Pastoral application.
- ¶11: Describes overcoming temptation through liturgical invocation: confession of sin, beseeching charity, calling assent/amen to annul 'the lust and the temptation.' References 'the elect' (institutional score 2.17). The power described operates through institutional practice (confession, communal prayer). Pastoral/institutional.
- ¶12: Continues prayer-over-person theme: 'a great power come to him out from the assent and the amen. It helps him.' Liturgical/pastoral application. Institutional score 4.35.
- ¶13: Very fragmentary (29 words). 'The assent and the amen go up to the ships' — references cosmic geography (ships of light), but in context of liturgical efficacy. The cosmic element is the destination of the liturgical act, not an independent cosmological mapping.
- ¶14: Extremely fragmentary (86 words but heavily lacunose). References 'the heavens and the ships' (cosmic geography) and 'the assent and the amen.' Seems to describe salvation/deliverance through liturgical invocation ('He is saved', 'they called over him'). Too fragmentary for confident classification but consistent with institutional/pastoral pattern of preceding paragraphs.
- ¶15: Very fragmentary (19 words). 'Behold' and 'your own cry' suggest direct address. Continues discussion of assent/amen. Too fragmentary for confident classification; classified as institutional consistent with surrounding context.
- ¶16: References 'the apostles, the first fathers' — institutional succession language. 'It might be uttered from God' and naming from apostles grounds the assent/amen in institutional authority. Fragmentary but clearly institutional.
- ¶17: Closing formula: 'Then, when the father [...] like this, he glorified and he [...].' Standard chapter-closing dialogue frame. 'The father' = editorial honorific for Mani. Very fragmentary (12 words).
